[00:31:16] Whereas on October 13th, 1775, the Continental Congress established the Continental Navy, marking the birth of what would become the United States Navy and setting our nation on a course towards maritime strength, security, and global leadership.

[00:31:33] And whereas for 250 years, the United States Navy has safeguarded the nation's security, supported global trade, fostered innovations, and promoted international stability through its worldwide presence. [00:32:29] And whereas the year 2025 marks the 250th anniversary of the United States Navy, a historic milestone that reflects two and a half centuries of sacrifice, innovation and steadfast service to the nation. So thank you. As mentioned, firefighter occupational cancer is now the leading cause of death among firefighters.

[01:01:04] That reality felt deeply in the Fairfax County Fire and Rescue Department. Scientific research underscores this danger. The International Agency for Research on Cancer recently classified the occupation itself as a Group 1 carcinogen. National studies further show that firefighters have a 9% higher risk of contracting cancer or the disease process itself and a 14% higher likelihood of dying from that cancer.

[01:01:28] The human impact is unmistakable. Approximately 40 members to date in the department are currently living with a cancer diagnosis. And in recent years, we have lost four courageous colleagues who are on the job to this disease. Captain Kimberly Shopa, firefighter Kathy Leary, Captain Barry Mayhem, as mentioned, and also tech technician Jaymon Cloyd.

[01:01:51] Through the department's Occupational Health Center, joint labor and management efforts, and leadership, we have deployed PSA, colorectal screening through our annual physicals, and more advanced screenings such as blood antigen tests and ultrasound-based early detection programs, program screening, which was very impactful.

[01:02:13] Over 1,500 firefighters, volunteer firefighters, and retirees were screened. The screenings identified 1,112 actual findings, including 236 high-risk conditions. Many were discovered before symptoms ever emerged, thyroid, kidney, etc., allowing for early intervention, significantly improving health outcomes, and then, in fact, saving lives.

[01:02:37] In response, Fairfax County Fire and Rescue has become a regional and national leader in early detection and cancer risk reduction. The department now provides annual comprehensive screenings, including cardiac and cancer-focused evaluations, in partnership with leading medical experts.

[01:02:54] The Fire and Rescue Department has also implemented wide-ranging policies to reduce hazards, exposures, clean cab gear practices, diesel exhaust mitigation, on-scene decontamination procedures, and modernized live fire training aligned with NFPA standards and continued investment in safer facilities, protective equipment, with data-driven health programs like WellFit.

[01:03:14] And thank you to you all. Fairfax County proudly supports national research initiatives such as National Fire Registry for Cancer, other university partners, and INOVA, recognizing that transparent reporting and long-term data are essential to improving outcomes for future generations.

[01:03:30] The Fire Rescue Department is honored to be designated as a National Fire Registry Gold Helmet Department for its leadership and participation. My next board matter is a joint board matter, and this is endorsement of the Metropolitan Washington Council of Governments and Washington Metropolitan Area Transit Authority Board's endorsement of the DMV Moves Plan. And you will see that this is done jointly with Supervisor Alcorn for his service on the WMATA board, Supervisor Lusk with his service on the COG board, and Supervisor Beerman as our transportation chair.

[01:59:10] And as the board is well aware, I served on the DMV Moves Task Force. I do want to thank, at this moment before I read this motion, I do want to thank our county executive because nobody in this room spent more time on the DMV moves matters than our county executive, Mr. Hill, did.

[01:59:29] And I want to acknowledge that and thank him because his guidance through this process has been integral in getting this item to the board today. On November 17, 2025, the boards of both COG and WMATA endorsed the DMV moves plan regarding public transportation in the national capital region.

[01:59:47] The DMV moves plan outlines the need for a long-term regionally coordinated approach to sustaining and modernizing transit services across the national capital region. This resolution acknowledges the critical role of transit in the region's economic vitality, mobility, and environmental goals, and recognizes that WMATA's major capital funding sources have lost significant purchasing power due to inflation and rising costs.

[02:00:11] Without new dedicated capital funding, WMATA will hit its debt capacity limit by FY 2029, forcing its capital program to shrink dramatically and resulting in long-term safety, reliability, and capacity challenges. The Virginia Railway Express and the region's local bus providers also face operating and maintenance pressures that threaten service levels.

[02:00:33] After extensive technical analysis, regional coordination, and public engagement, the DMV Moves Regional Transportation Investment and Action Plan was developed. The plan outlines a 25-year investment framework, identifies long-term capital and operating needs, and recommends funding strategies to support an integrated, reliable, and financially sustainable regional transit network.

[02:00:56] The resolution also calls on federal partners to increase funding, particularly restoring Passenger Rail Investment and Improvement Act purchasing power and directs WMATA to aggressively pursue federal grants, loans, and financing opportunities. Finally, COG and WMATA called on elected officials, agencies, and regional stakeholders to support commitments needed to implement the DMV MOVE's plan and ensure a stable, integrated, and sustainable future for the transit systems that underpin the region's economic and social prosperity. Therefore, I move the Board of Supervisors endorse the resolution passed by the COG and WMATA Boards that endorses the DMV moves plan.

[02:30:59] Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I'll start with my first motion. which is for a sewer lateral board matter. Many of the residents have privately owned sanitary sewer laterals. They extend from their home into the VDOT right-of-way before connecting to the publicly maintained sanitary sewer.

[02:31:20] When such private laterals are damaged or require replacement, homeowners are often surprised to learn that the financial responsibility is theirs and that repairing pipes located within the VDOT right-of-way can be difficult to permit and expensive to construct. Residents within the Frank County District have shared that the county code provisions of Prince William and Loudoun County are such that local government either owns or maintains and manages the laterals when located within the VDOT right-of-way, thereby potentially reducing the cost burden to their individual homeowners.

[02:31:56] Since earlier this year, staff from the Department of Public Works and Environmental Services and staff from land development services have been working very closely with two affected property owners in my district and staff from my office on this issue. DPWES staff notes that Fairfax County Code Section 65-6-5 ownership of building sewer explicitly establishes that the county does not own or maintain these private laterals even under public rights of way. The county currently manages more than 3,300 miles of public sanitary sewer. Significant expansion

[02:32:36] of the county's maintenance responsibilities to include all private laterals would be prohibitively expensive to the county sewer customers, although an immediate solution to address unique situations where property owners are at no fault of their own become disproportionately impacted should be explored. As such, I move to the county executive, direct staff in the Department of Public Works and Environmental Services in coordination with Land Development Services and the Office of the County Attorney to do the following.

[02:33:07] First, estimate the scale of the problem in which privately owned sewer laterals are located within the VDOT right-of-way. Second, research how neighboring jurisdictions have addressed this particular problem. Third, identify alternatives available to the county to assist residents in these situations along with the potential benefits, implementation timeline, and costs, including additional employee resources necessary for each alternative, and then fourth, provide a summary report of staff findings, including their recommendations to the Board of Supervisors.

[02:33:44] I further move that after staff have provided such summary report to the Board of Supervisors and the county executive direct staff to initiate the minimum necessary amendments to the county code that would not have a substantial adverse impact on county sewer charges in order to implement the staff recommendation.

[02:34:02] So moved, Mr. Chairman. [04:20:50] Niebauer from the Department of Planning and Development Fairfax County is home to seven manufactured housing communities with 1,769 units unlike traditional home ownership where the land and home are owned by a single entity residents of manufactured homes typically own the home but rent the land from a separate entity.

[04:21:14] These communities serve as a crucial source of housing for working families, but the supply has been diminishing over time. In 2021, the board established the Manufactured Housing Task Force, which developed as a set of recommendations aimed at preserving affordable housing opportunities that exist within manufactured housing communities. Three of The 34 task force recommendations relate to the comprehensive plan.

[04:21:42] One, define manufactured housing communities. Two, provide area plan recommendations regarding preservation and if redevelopment is planned, achieving the goal of no net loss through replacement considerations. And three, strengthen policy plan language to have the same protection as other single-family residential communities.

[04:22:02] In a collaborative approach effort with housing and community development, We engaged property owners and managers, community members, advocates, the Manufactured Housing Roundtable, Planning Commission, and the Board of Supervisors Land Use Committees.

[04:22:20] There was a second round of engagement to review the draft recommendations, which was published in both Spanish and English. The community members expressed support for preservation strategies and concerns and fear that redevelopment will occur and result in displacement.

[04:22:37] The property owners and managers stated that there are currently no pending applications to replan, rezone, or redevelop any of the communities. The adopted Appendix 10, Guidelines for Mobile Home Retention in the Land Use Policy Plan, lacks guidance on preservation strategies for existing manufactured housing communities and preserving affordability levels.

[04:23:01] To strengthen the policy plan language, the proposed updates would remove Appendix 10 and create a new Objective 7 within the housing element and include policies that preserve existing manufactured housing communities through facilitating potential investments in infrastructure, encourage rezoning or special exception to support preservation, and promote resident-led acquisition or non-profit organization when the communities are for sale.

[04:23:28] If redevelopment occurs, proposed policies include preserving existing affordability levels with a goal of one-for-one replacement and, if feasible, relocating community members back on site into affordable committed units. Developers should consult with the Department of Housing and Community Development early in the process, as well as conduct an affordability analysis and review relocation support per the manufactured housing relocation guidelines.

[04:23:56] When redevelopment or other environmental hazards warrant relocation, identify land to support relocation and preservation of affordability, and facilitate home ownership opportunities. The comprehensive plan references the Manufactured Housing Relocation Guidelines.

[04:24:13] While this item is not part of the comprehensive plan amendment, it will be put forward for consideration as an action item and provide tools and recommendations for residents to find temporary and permanent relocation support. The proposed updates look at providing preservation guidance for each community within the area plan.

[04:24:32] Woodley Hills, Audubon, and Waple Mills Estates are currently zoned for residential manufactured home park district. The proposed text is to be explicit that these are planned and developed for manufactured housing use and should remain. Some units within Audubon Estates are located within the floodplain.

[04:24:51] Replacement of units within the floodplain needs to follow floodplain regulations per their zoning ordinance. The comprehensive plan land use plan map density range will also be updated to reflect the existing density. Meadows of Chantilly is zoned for industrial use.

[04:25:08] However, the adopted plan supports the community as a suitable residential neighborhood that should be protected. The plan-based map will not change at this time, given that the plan recommendations surrounding the site are industrial and the areas within the airport noise district.

[04:25:24] Harmony Place Mobile Home Park is zoned for commercial use, but is planned for residential use. Replacement of units within the floodplain should follow floodplain regulations per their zoning ordinance. The proposed revision would note that these are planned and developed as manufactured housing use, and if redeveloped, the goal of one-for-one replacement and relocation guidelines should be met.

[04:25:49] Alexandria Mobile Home Park and PennDaw Terrace Mobile Home Park are also zoned for commercial use and have mixed-use redevelopment options per the Richmond Highway Corridor Plan. The proposed revisions include updating the plan recommendations to know that these are communities that are planned and developed as a manufactured housing use at the baseline, but also retain the redevelopment options currently within the plan.

[04:26:12] If redeveloped, the goal of one-for-one replacement and relocation guidelines should be met. That concludes the presentation for the comprehensive plan, and now I'm going to pass it on to Janann to go over the Zoning Ordinance Amendment.

[04:26:53] Good afternoon.

[04:27:22] My name is Janann Cato, and I'm a planner with the Zoning Administration Division. And I'll be presenting today on the proposed zoning ordinance amendment, but I was told to wait a second, so I'm going to wait.

[04:27:56] Okay, so I'll be presenting today on the proposed zoning ordinance amendment. My name is Jananne Cato. Three of the seven manufactured home parks are zoned to the RMHP district. Three parks are zoned C8, and one is zoned I3.

[04:28:14] The parks in the C8 and I3 districts are legally nonconforming uses. As part of the coordinated community outreach, we solicited feedback from residents on minimum separation distances between homes with a voting poster at each meeting.

[04:28:34] Participants generally prefer to maintain the current requirements of 15 feet between units. We also were concerned about limited parking and a desire for more spaces to accommodate the number of people in each household. The proposed changes to district standards are intended to bring the existing parks closer to conformance.

[04:28:56] These changes do not require any modifications to the existing legally non-conforming manufactured homes, but would apply in the future or to existing unpermitted additions. The main changes include increasing density from 6 to 10 units per acre, which is consistent with the density in the zoning ordinance at the time when most of the parks were developed.

[04:29:19] Reducing setbacks to the boundary of the park to 5 feet, which better aligns with existing conditions and with certain other residential districts. Also reducing certain minimum required distances between structures by maintaining the 15-foot minimum for side-to-side separation between units, but reducing the separation distance to 10 feet if units are oriented end-to-side or end-to-end, which you could see in the diagram on the bottom right.

[04:29:48] The advertised range does go down to 10 feet, eight feet, and six feet, as that's the minimum required by building code for manufactured homes. Allowing roofed or open decks to be as close as five feet to another structure. This is to address that many of the homes already have covered decks or patios that don't meet current requirements and cannot be permitted.

[04:30:12] Increasing the parking rate from 1.5 to two spaces per unit, which aligns more with the parking requirement for single-family detached dwellings. And this would not require changes to existing parking, but would apply to any additional development. The proposed amendment would also allow for the board to modify the RMHP district standards with the approval of profit conditions or a special exception.

[04:30:43] The amendment also proposes two new application types and expands a current application. Currently, the zoning administrator can approve an error in building location up to 10%. The proposed amendment adds that distance between manufactured homes can qualify for the same administrative approval.

[04:31:03] Also being proposed is a new special permit process that would allow homeowners to request a reduction in the minimum required distance between manufactured homes down to either the building code minimum or five feet, whichever is greater. This provides flexibility for existing homes that either have or would like an addition or deck while still maintaining safety.

[04:31:26] Additionally, the amendment includes a new special exception application for non-conforming parks that would allow the parks to reconfigure or otherwise make updates to the site. As part of the review, the board would have the ability to modify the RMHP district standards, and so the park would no longer be considered non-conforming.

[04:31:50] Finally, there are two more proposed changes that we'd like to highlight. State code dictates that manufactured homes are eligible to be replaced with a similar unit. Currently, there are a number of homes located in the floodplain that are considered legally non-conforming, and under current zoning ordinance regulations, their replacement requires a special exception. The amendment updates the floodplain section to allow these homes to be replaced without a special exception, which is consistent with state code. Replacement units would still be required to be elevated for safety. Also, additions to units or new pad sites in the floodplain

[04:32:30] would require a special exception approval. Lastly, the transitional screening requirement is proposed to be revised to group manufactured homes with other single-family detached and attached dwellings by removing the requirement for a 25-foot buffer and 6-foot solid fence or barrier between manufactured homes and single-family homes.

[05:33:29] Good evening, Mr. Chairman and members of the Board. My name is Arpita Chatterjee, Transportation Planner with the County's Department of Transportation. The purpose of this PA is to update the comprehensive plan guidance for the corridor based on the findings of the Route 29 study.

[05:33:47] The Route 29 study was undertaken to reassess the long-term needs of the corridor and develop context-sensitive solutions that serve residents and commuters. Currently, adopted comprehensive plan shows planned interchanges at Legato Road, Monument Drive, and Shirley Gate-Vapalsmill Road at Route 29.

[05:34:04] The study corridor falls within the Fairfax Centre area. Our recommendation is in line with what the plan recommends for the FCA. The study looked at three different scenarios. Existing conditions, future 2045 baseline conditions, assuming we make no improvements to the corridor, and future 2045 preferred mitigation alternative.

[05:34:25] This was the basis for the recommended changes to the comprehensive plan. It includes removing the three interchanges and proposed ad-grade improvements. We had extensive public outreach throughout the course of the study and the plan amendment. We conducted two public surveys and we found that the community supports staff recommendation for the removal of the planned interchanges and have ad-grade improvements.

[05:34:47] Here is a quick rundown of the recommended ad-grade improvements. For the intersection of Vapilsmill-Shirley Gate Road at Route 29, our study found that it has high volumes, delays, long queues, and lacks adequate active transportation infrastructure. To address these issues, our study proposed the addition of a second left-hand lane for both Shirley Gate Road and Vapilsmill Road, signal timing adjustment, high visibility crosswalks at all four legs of the intersection, median refuge islands, etc.

[05:35:15] These recommended improvements addressed both traffic operations as well as pedestrians and bicyclists' safety and comfort. The intersection of Lagarto Road and Monument Drive did not show significant operational deficiencies in the existing and the baseline scenarios.

[05:35:31] With minor lane configuration changes, signal timing optimization, both the intersections, Lagarto Road and Monument Drive, are expected to operate with fewer delays throughout the peak period. The study evaluated the existing pedestrian level of comfort and bicycle level of traffic stress for the study corridor.

[05:35:48] Based on our study findings, we see that existing infrastructure is not conducive for pedestrians and bicyclists' safety and comfort. We are making recommendations to improve the facility, especially on the southern side. So in conclusion, staff recommends retaining the earth-grade configuration of the corridor and remove the planned interchanges.

[05:36:07] Staff also recommends, as an interim condition on the south side of Route 29, to facilitate the planned goal of shared-use paths. [05:40:44] And this, of course, is dealing with a public hearing on a proposed zoning ordinance amendment regarding electrical substations. And at this time, I would recognize our county staff for a staff presentation. Thank you. I'm Carmen Bishop in the Zoning Administration Division, and I'm joined by William Malin, Assistant Zoning Administrator, and Tracy Strunk, Director of the Department of Planning and Development, and I'll be presenting the proposed zoning ordinance amendment for electrical substations.

[05:41:17] The proposed amendment focuses on changes to permissions, new setbacks and screening standards, a noise study requirement, and the revisions to the alternative site review standard. The proposed text also includes various minor edits and organizational changes.

[05:41:40] Given the need for substations to be located throughout the county, the proposed amendment does not remove any zoning districts where substations are currently allowed and would add the PCC district. The primary change in permissions would be to change the substations from by right to requiring an SE in two districts, the I-3 and the I-4 district.

[05:42:05] This change would align with the permissions more closely with the permissions for data centers. Substations are already in SE use in most zoning districts, and the SE process is processed concurrently with the 2232 requirement.

[05:42:22] The Planning Commission recommended also requiring an SE in the I-5 and I-6 when located next to residential. The amendment includes proposed setback and screening standards to assist with mitigating visual and noise impacts of substations on surrounding areas.

[05:42:42] The proposed standards would apply to new substations and also to an existing substation, but only in the direction of the expansion and only if the expansion is more than 10 percent. The amendment also includes setbacks 100 feet from residential, 50 feet from a public or private street, and 20 feet from commercial.

[05:43:09] The Planning Commission recommended a setback of 200 feet from residential and 30 feet from commercial, where a substation abuts a street or a residential area, a minimum 12-foot-high visually solid fence or wall would be required. A landscape buffer would also be required, which could be located within the setback area.

[05:43:30] And the planting requirements for that would be similar to what we have in the zoning ordinance for transitional screening, but would not require deciduous trees. Substations are also subject to the county's noise ordinance.

[05:43:46] As part of the background research on this amendment, a report was prepared by an acoustical consultant. This consultant noted that the sound levels of the equipment, which is primarily a hum from the transformers, can range from 55 up to 80 decibels at the source, which could potentially exceed the maximum allowed under the noise ordinance of 55 decibels at night or 60 decibels during the day in residential areas.

[05:44:12] Each site is unique with many variables that affect sound propagation, therefore the amendment proposes to require a noise study, but only for substations that are abutting residential to demonstrate that the proposed substation would meet the noise ordinance.

[05:44:29] The amendment also includes edits to the current special exception standard that requires a review of alternative sites. This standard applies to proposed light utility facilities in residential districts and requires a finding that there isn't an alternative site in a commercial or an industrial district.

[05:44:49] The proposed edits would allow consideration of co-location or expansion of an existing site if that would minimize adverse effects on the residential district. The edits also expand the review area for distribution substations from 500 feet to one mile consistent with the current requirement for transmission substations.

[05:45:11] The Planning Commission recommended additional edits to this provision to include consideration of properties abutting or across the street from residential. Thank you, and we are happy to answer questions. Thank you very much. Let me ask if anyone has any questions at this time.

[05:45:28] We'll have another chance, of course, after the public hearing. Any questions at this time? Supervisor Alcorn. Yeah, thank you, Mr. Chairman. [07:03:45] Welcome. Good evening. My name is Douglas Bell, and I've previously testified before this board and the Planning Commission regarding the data center zoning amendment.

[07:04:01] I currently serve as Senior Principal Consultant and President of Kavanaugh-Tachi, an acoustical consulting firm founded in 1975. In the course of my nearly 40-year career, I've consulted on over 1,000 environmental noise projects.

[07:04:17] Additionally, I co-authored a textbook on the topic of industrial noise control. As a subject matter expert in environmental noise, I'm speaking to offer further insights. The proposed amendment includes various design restrictions targeting potential noise impacts associated with substations.

[07:04:37] Specifically, these restrictions include imposing increased setbacks and requiring solid fencing surrounding the substation. It is my opinion that these restrictions will only impose a burden on utilities without achieving any of the intended goals. Currently, noise emitted from a substation is controlled by the Fairfax County Noise Ordinance.

[07:04:58] This is a robust and unambiguous ordinance which has served the county for decades. It's crucial to understand that the limits are determined by receiving property boundaries and receiving property use, ensuring that residential districts are well protected.

[07:05:15] The ordinance even accounts for heightened awareness during nighttime hours by applying the most restrictive limits during this period. It is my professional opinion these standards are equitable and practical. The zoning amendment recommends increasing setbacks and providing mandatory solid fence screening around substations.

[07:05:34] Since the regulatory limits of the noise ordinance already dictate acoustic design, these requirements provide no additional benefit to the community. The property line limits defined in the ordinance must be met, regardless of what the setbacks are.

[07:05:52] It's my experience that when a noise ordinance exists, utilities will consult with acoustic engineers to ensure that their projects comply with applicable limits. In this process, acoustic engineers will use several effective tools to estimate noise impacts and then provide recommendations to mitigate impacts when warranted.

[07:06:10] There are other options besides setbacks and screening that can be used to control substation sound. To arbitrarily dictate setbacks and screening to control noise could result in very inefficient designs. In the course of preparing for this testimony, I reviewed a report entitled Electrical Substations in BESS, Noise Findings and Recommendations.

[07:06:33] This report was prepared for the Zoning Administration Division and dated April 30th, 2025. The report does not appear to recommend additional acoustic restrictions that should be applied to substations besides enforcing the existing ordinance. In fact, it specifically points out that setback distances are not a viable measure to ensure sound levels.

[07:06:55] It is my experience that industrial projects are successful in balancing the objectives of developers with the need of an acceptable sound environment for the community when three factors are part of the development process. First, the community has clearly defined objective standards to serve as goals for the developers to achieve.

[07:07:15] Second, the developer commissions a sound study to determine the feasibility of a design with regard to achieving these goals. And third, post-construction sound measurements are performed to verify performance and to identify any problems to be remediated. In conclusion, it is my opinion that with regard to sound produced by substations, The Fairfax County community is best served by addressing noise through the framework of the existing noise regulation.

[07:07:41] This regulation requires developers to perform a comprehensive evaluation of acoustic impacts in order to ensure compliance with standards which are appropriate and effective. Additional regulatory requirements with minimum setbacks and required screening do not provide additional noise benefits to the community.

[07:13:50] Are you here? Okay. Good evening. Chairman McKay, members of the board, thank you for this opportunity to speak. My name is William Deegan, and I represent Critical Power Consulting, a firm that supports major development projects, secure the electrical capacity they require by coordinating with utilities across the nation. [07:14:29] express our strong opposition to the opposed substation ZOAM and the Planning Commission's recommendations. Utility development is already heavily regulated. Substation siting and construction are governed by a comprehensive regulatory framework at the local, state, and federal levels. Each is designed to ensure safety, reliability, and public accountability. At the State Corporation Commission, the CPCN process requires utilities to evaluate co-location options, upgrades to existing substations, and multiple transmission line routing alternatives.

[07:15:05] These cases are highly technical and data-driven. They are open to public comment and require a demonstrated need for the capacity requested. At the federal level, the FERC and the NERC impose reliability standards for system planning, contingency performance, and infrastructure adequacy.

[07:15:25] Utilities cannot deviate from these standards. They must build the infrastructure required to meet forecasted load while maintaining a stabilized grid. In short, substations are already among the most scrutinized infrastructure assets in the country.

[07:15:41] The ZOAM does not fill a regulatory gap. It adds a redundant layer that conflicts with established regulatory pathways. The ZOAM's criteria are overly restrictive and functionally unworkable. Fairfax County already utilizes the 2232 review and the site plan process to review location, screening, compatibility, and community impacts.

[07:16:04] This proposed ZOAM introduces expanded setbacks, heightened screening, and special exemption requirements that significantly reduce the number of feasible parcels, particularly in industrial districts where new capacity must be added.

[07:16:20] When land options shrink, utilities are forced onto compact parcels that can only accommodate gas-insulated substations, also known as GIS. GIS is a valuable technology, however, it costs two to four times the traditional air-insulated designs.

[07:16:37] They are more expensive to maintain, and they require specialized training to ensure reliable grid operations. Mandating GIS through zoning and not engineering creates avoidable cost escalation, and these additional costs will not solely be absorbed by the large load customers as discussed.

[07:16:58] Comments made during the Planning Commission hearing suggested that large load customers shift substation costs onto others. This is incorrect. Large load customers already pay the full substation infrastructure required to serve their load, along with any needed transmission or distribution upgrades.

[07:17:17] However, when substations are needed to support the broader grid, not the large load customer, the ZOAM creates new challenges. New challenges that restrict viable sites and effectively force GIS-only solutions.

[07:17:33] This adds system-wide costs that will ultimately flow down to the residents, small businesses, and community institutions of Fairfax. The ZOAM is also going to slow down substation delivery and possibly undermine reliability. A typical substation already takes 6 to 10 years from planning to energization.

[07:17:53] In practice, the ZOAM delays projects precisely when the county is working to expand housing, reduce fossil fuel use in the homes, electrify fleets, enable transit charging, implement the CCAP, and operational energy strategy commitments.

[07:18:11] Electrification cannot occur without capacity. Capacity cannot be added without substations. As drafted, the ZOAM creates a structural barrier to meeting the county's own energy and economic development goals. Critical power consulting respectfully urges the board to reject or substantially revise the ZOAM. The amendment does not improve safety or compatibility but what it does do is it reduces site feasibility, increases community costs, and impedes the timely development of essential grid infrastructure. Fairfax County's growth,

[07:18:49] reliability, and decarbonization efforts all depend on a predictable, workable path for substation development. The ZOAM, in its current form, does not provide that path. [08:13:03] So I thank you for your time. Thank you. Our next speaker is Holden Foxworth. Chairman McKay, members of the board, thank you for the opportunity to speak. Substations are critical public infrastructure, facilities that power every economic sector in Fairfax County.

[08:13:23] They are not optional amenities. They are the cornerstone of a modern grid. Over the past decade, the process of delivering a substation has become increasingly difficult. At the same time, demand for reliable electricity continues to increase. Fairfax County's own plans, CCAP, the Operational Energy Strategy, the Countywide Strategic Plan, all recognize that the county's climate goals require more substation distribution capacity, not less.

[08:13:50] To understand the real-world impact of the proposed OAM, we conducted a countywide land analysis of industrial parcels. Across I-3 through I-6 districts, 124 parcels initially appear viable for substation siting.

[08:14:06] Once known development activity, parcel geometry, and the proposed ZOAM criteria are applied, that number drops to just 12 parcels, less than 10% of the available inventory. This leaves the county with a critically thin pipeline for the infrastructure required to support housing growth, electrification, fleet transition, EV charging, and economic development.

[08:14:30] If industrial zoning is restricted to the same degree as residential, substations will inevitably start showing up in residential areas. The ZOAM as drafted makes that outcome more likely. To be clear, this is a county-wide conversation concerning substations and should not be misconstrued as an opportunity to single out any one use.

[08:14:51] There has been testimony suggesting that Fairfax County residents will shoulder the costs for substation projects driven by data centers. That is incorrect. Data centers pay for their own infrastructure. They fund substation construction, transmission extensions, and distribution improvements required to energize their facilities.

[08:15:10] Large load customers enter binding financial agreements with utilities long before the project reaches the 2232 application. They post major upfront deposits and assume long-term minimum demand obligations that ensure they, not Fairfax residents, cover the cost of system upgrades.

[08:15:29] If a data center needs a substation, a transmission line, or a distribution upgrade, the JLARC study confirms the data center pays for it. However, for substation servicing non-data center uses, of which the county will need many over the next 10 years, any excess costs will be borne directly by the utility and passed through to the rate base.

[08:15:51] Fairfax County is aiming for a cleaner, electrified, economically competitive future. That future is impossible without substations. The ZOAM, as drafted, significantly limits where substations can go and contradicts the county's own climate, electrification, and growth strategies.

[08:16:08] I respectfully ask the board to refine the amendment so it preserves flexibility for substations within industrial districts and protects communities without constraining the infrastructure the entire county depends on. [08:40:42] regulation and at this time i would recognize staff for a presentation good evening mr chairman and members of the board for the record my name is john friedman i'm representing the department of Land Development Services. With me this evening are Kirsten Munst, Director of the Site Development and Inspection Divisions of LDS, and Erin Blanche with the County Attorney's Office.

[08:41:06] For your considerations this evening are proposed amendments to the Erosion and Stormwater Management Ordinance. The proposed amendments implement changes to the Virginia Erosion and Stormwater Management Regulations, in particular changes to the water quality control requirements.

[08:41:22] The amendments also include clarifications of existing ordinance provisions. The proposed amendments required by changes to the state regulation are mandatory on localities and have to be incorporated into our local ordinance.

[08:41:39] To comply with the effective data of the changes to the state regulation, staff begin enforcing the new water quality control requirements this past July 1st. Construction plans submitted before the effective date may continue to be processed under the old criteria to final plan approval.

[08:41:57] We have not experienced any issues to date with application of the new requirements. The major changes to the criteria are as follows. The allowable phosphorus load from land-disturbing activity for new development is reduced from 0.41 pounds per acre per year to 0.26 pounds per acre per year for plans submitted on or after July 1, 2025.

[08:42:25] The allowable phosphorus load from redevelopment is unchanged. Compliance with the water quality control criteria for both new development and redevelopment must be demonstrating using the updated Virginia runoff reduction method, which includes changes to the amount of phosphorus wash-off from various land cover types.

[08:42:48] Water quality controls and erosion and sediment controls must be designed in accordance with the updated edition of the Virginia Stormwater Management Handbook. The changes to the method for determining compliance somewhat offset the reduction in the allowable phosphorus discharge.

[08:43:07] The end result is that the phosphorus reductions for sites with higher proportion of maintained lawns will rise slightly, and the required reduction for sites with a higher proportion of impervious area will be slightly lower. That concludes my presentation. I'll be happy to answer any questions you may have.

The Virginia Code enables Fairfax County to regulate trespass towing and certain fees. Trespass towing services is the removal of vehicles parked in violation of law or rules set forth by a property owner manager lessee or authorized agent thereof without the consent of the vehicle owner approximately 45 000 trespass toes occur in fairfax county

[08:49:29] annually by 16 trespass toe operators this public hearing considers amendments to fairfax county's Trespass Tow Ordinance, which is found at Fairfax County Code, Section 82532. These proposed amendments were initiated by the Fairfax County Trespass Towing Board and include the following.

[08:49:50] Replace the registration certificate with a requirement for trespass tow operators to obtain an operator permit from our agency to tow and store or release vehicles within the county, provide for the denial, suspension, or revocation of operator permits, changes to certain rates and charges and minor changes in editorial corrections. Staff recommends the following regarding certain rates and charges. And apologies for the lengths, there are several proposed increases. Number one, establishing a hookup and initial towing fee of 210 for passenger

[08:50:28] cars. Number two, increasing the hookup and initial towing fee for vehicles other than passenger cars with a gross vehicle weight rating, also known as GVWR, of less than 7,500 pounds from $150 to $210, increasing the hookup and initial towing fee for vehicles other than passenger cars with a GVWR of 7,501 pounds through 10,000 pounds from $250 to $305.

[08:51:00] Number four, increasing the hookup and initial towing fee for vehicles other than passenger cars with a GVWR of greater than 2,000 pounds from $500 to $610. Number five, increasing the drop fee from $50 to $80.

[08:51:16] Number six, increasing the storage fee after 24 hours from $50 per 24-hour period to $61 per 24-hour period. And last but not least, at number seven, increasing the administrative fee from $75 to $92.

[08:51:34] These proposed rates and charges are consistent with the Virginia State Corporation Commission's recommendations, fee maximums provided for in the Virginia Code, and or the BLS Index, which has historically been used by our team in DCCS as the formula and methodology for trespass towing rate analysis and recommendations.

First, I would like to thank you for the opportunity to serve on the Trespass Towing Advisory Board with other outstanding members. Collectively, members of the Advisory Board represent 72 years of experience in the towing industry, 32 years of experience as members of the Advisory Board,

[09:07:38] 23 years of experience in the enforcement of towing laws and 12 years of experience in regulating the trespass towing industry in Fairfax County. The advisory board has a deep understanding of the trespass towing industry and the appropriate regulations that tow operators must follow and the rates necessary to sustain the industry which provides vital parking enforcement services to private property owners countywide. Over the course of seven months, the advisory board worked to develop the proposed revisions in Chapter 825-32.

[09:08:12] The most significant proposed revision is the introduction of operator permits, which, if approved, will exercise the authority granted to the county by the Virginia General Assembly in their 2024 legislative session. And that authority allows the county or allows localities in Planning District 8 to require tow operators to obtain a permit to tow and store vehicles within the locality.

[09:08:39] This permit would replace the current tow operator registration requirement and would enable the Department of Cable and Consumer Services to take important regulatory actions against trespass tow operators that violate county tow regulations. This expanded regulatory authority over the trespass towing industry would protect the public from unscrupulous trespass tow operators.

[09:09:01] The next significant proposed revision is an increase to trespass tow rates and fees. In addition to county code, tow rates are also prescribed by the Code of Virginia in Section 46.2-1233, which sets the maximum allowable fee that can be charged for the hookup and towing of passenger vehicles at $210.

[09:09:21] The Virginia General Assembly increased the maximum allowable rate to $210 pursuant to recommendations by the State Corporation Commission, which was directed by the General Assembly in 2024 to undertake an examination of towing rates in Virginia.

[09:09:39] In November 2024, the SEC issued a 128-page report of their findings that provides a comprehensive assessment of the cost incurred by trespass tow operators and recommends the appropriate transportation cost index elements to be considered in tow ratemaking.

[09:09:56] It was the conclusion of the SEC that the appropriate rate for the hookup and tow of a passenger vehicle from private property should be set between $200 and $210, And they also found that the drop fee of $25 prescribed by Virginia Code is too low and should be set at 40 to 50 percent of the trespass towing rate.

[09:10:16] In determining the appropriate rates and fees for Fairfax County, the advisory board took the SEC report findings, the maximum rates set by the Virginia General Assembly, and the rates set by Fairfax County in its contract with tow operators for public safety towing into consideration.

[09:10:35] The rate set forth by Fairfax County Contract for Public Safety Towing sets the non-accident hookup and tow rate for vehicles over 7,500 pounds at $464, and for vehicles over 10,000 pounds at $628.

[09:10:54] Additional allowable contract fees include $87 per day for storage that applies immediately when the towed vehicle arrives at the tow lot and a $165 administrative fee that is charged after the vehicle has been in the possession of the tow operator for more than three days.

[09:11:11] The rates proposed by the advisory board for vehicles over 7,500 pounds and 10,000 pounds and the advisory board's proposal to increase the storage fee and the administrative fee were guided by and were reflective of the county contract rates to bring trespass tow operators to parity with public safety towing.

[09:11:37] I must note, however, that the staff recommended rates and charges differs from the rates and charges recommended by the advisory board. It was the advisory board's belief that the TTAB proposal was justified, and that is the proposal that the trespass-to-advisory board voted on unanimously.
